Let's try to solve from first principles. We need an efficient algorithm for N up to 5e5. The operation is local and reversible. We can think of the string as a set of "particles". A known approach for such block swap problems is to map the string to a sequence of "differences" or "transitions". For example, consider the positions where the character changes. Let the positions of transitions be p_1, p_2, ..., p_{k-1}. The operation A on i: it requires that the block from i to i+X+Y-1 has exactly one transition? Actually, the pattern "0^X 1^Y" has exactly one transition (from 0 to 1) inside the block. After operation, it becomes "1^Y 0^X", which also has exactly one transition (from 1 to 0). So the number of transitions inside the block changes from 1 to 1, but the position of the transition moves from i+X-1 (the last 0) to i+Y-1 (the last 1). So the transition moves left by X? Actually, before: transition at i+X-1 (0 to 1). After: transition at i+Y-1 (1 to 0). Since Y = (X+Y) - X, the new transition is at i+Y-1 = i + (M - X) - 1 = i + M - X - 1. The old transition was at i+X-1. So it moved from i+X-1 to i+M-X-1, which is a shift of (M-2X). If M = X+Y, then M-2X = Y-X. So the transition moves by Y-X positions to the right (if Y>X) or left (if Y<X). This suggests that the positions of transitions modulo something might be invariant.

More precisely, consider the set of positions (0-indexed) where S_i != S_{i+1} for i=0..N-2. Let's call them "cuts". The operation A on i: it requires that S_i..S_{i+X-1} are 0, S_{i+X}..S_{i+X+Y-1} are 1. So there is a cut at position i+X-1 (between 0 and 1). Also, just before i, there might be a 1 (if i>0 and S_{i-1}=1), and just after i+X+Y-1, there might be a 0 (if i+X+Y < N and S_{i+X+Y}=0). After operation, the block becomes ones then zeros. So the effect on the cuts: the cut at i+X-1 is removed (since the boundary between the new ones and zeros is at i+Y-1, but the old cut at i+X-1 disappears because the character at i+X-1 changes from 0 to 1? S_1,S_2 are 0, S_3 is 1. Operation: S_1 becomes 1, S_2,S_3 become 0. So S_2 (which is i+X-1) becomes 0. So the character at i+X-1 changes from 0 to 0? Actually before: 0, after: 0. So the cut at i+X-1 (between 0 and 1) might be removed if the adjacent characters also change. Let's trace the cuts. Before: at i-1: if S_{i-1}=1, then cut at i-1 (1|0). At i+X-1: cut (0|1). At i+X+Y-1: if S_{i+X+Y}=0, cut at i+X+Y-1 (1|0). After: S_i..S_{i+Y-1}=1, S_{i+Y}..S_{i+X+Y-1}=0. So now: at i-1: S_{i-1}=1, S_i=1, so no cut if there was one? Actually, if there was a cut at i-1 (1|0), it becomes 1|1, so cut removed. At i+Y-1: S_{i+Y-1}=1, S_{i+Y}=0, so new cut at i+Y-1 (1|0). At i+X+Y-1: S_{i+X+Y-1}=0, S_{i+X+Y}=0 (if existed), so cut removed. Also, the old cut at i+X-1: before, S_{i+X-1}=0, S_{i+X}=1, so cut (0|1). After: S_{i+X-1}=0 (if Y < X) or 1 (if Y > X)? Let's be systematic.

Let M = X+Y. Operation A: on [i, i+M-1], we have 0^X 1^Y. After: 1^Y 0^X. So the block is reversed in terms of character order? Actually, it's a swap of the two parts. Let's think about a different invariant. The operation is essentially moving a 0 to the right by Y, and a 1 to the left by X, but they are coupled. If we consider the "center of mass" of zeros, maybe it's invariant modulo something.

Consider the sum over all zeros of their positions, and sum over all ones of their positions. Let P0 = sum_{p: S_p=0} p, P1 = sum_{p: S_p=1} p. Under operation A, the zeros at i..i+X-1 (positions) become ones, and the ones at i+X..i+X+Y-1 become zeros. So the new P0' = P0 - sum(i..i+X-1) + sum(i+X..i+X+Y-1). The new P1' = P1 - sum(i+X..i+X+Y-1) + sum(i..i+X-1). So P0' - P1' = (P0 - P1) - 2[sum(i..i+X-1) - sum(i+X..i+X+Y-1)]. Compute the sum difference: sum_{k=0}^{X-1} (i+k) - sum_{l=0}^{Y-1} (i+X+l) = X i + X(X-1)/2 - [ Y i + Y X + Y(Y-1)/2 ] = (X-Y) i + [X(X-1) - Y X - Y(Y-1)]/2 = (X-Y) i + [X^2 - X - 2XY - Y^2 + Y]/2. This is not constant. So P0 - P1 is not invariant.

What about the sum of positions of zeros modulo (X+Y)? Let's compute the change in P0 modulo M. The change is: ΔP0 = sum(i+X..i+X+Y-1) - sum(i..i+X-1). This is exactly the difference above. Modulo M, we can simplify. Note that the two intervals are adjacent: [i, i+X-1] and [i+X, i+X+Y-1] = [i+X, i+M-1]. So the union is [i, i+M-1]. The sum of positions in the union is sum_{k=0}^{M-1} (i+k) = M i + M(M-1)/2. The sum of the first part is sum_{k=0}^{X-1} (i+k) = X i + X(X-1)/2. So the sum of the second part is M i + M(M-1)/2 - [X i + X(X-1)/2] = (M-X) i + M(M-1)/2 - X(X-1)/2 = Y i + M(M-1)/2 - X(X-1)/2. So ΔP0 = (second) - (first) = Y i + M(M-1)/2 - X(X-1)/2 - X i - X(X-1)/2 = (Y-X) i + M(M-1)/2 - X(X-1). This is linear in i, so modulo M, it depends on i mod M. So P0 mod M is not invariant.

So sum of remainders is invariant? Let's check: original zero runs: [3] -> sum of remainders mod 2: 3 mod 2 = 1. New zero runs: [1,2] -> remainders: 1, 0. Sum = 1. What about ones runs? Original: [2] -> remainder mod 1: 2 mod 1 = 0. New ones runs: [1,1] -> remainders: 0,0. Sum = 0. Sum of remainders mod 2: 1+0=1. Original zero run: "000" -> length 3, remainder 1. So sum remained 1. One runs: original "111" -> length 3, remainder 0. New one runs: "1" (len 1, rem 0) and "11" (len 2, rem 0). Sum 0. So it seems the sum of lengths of zero runs modulo X, or equivalently the sum of remainders, is invariant? Since total zeros is invariant, the sum of remainders modulo X is invariant? Not exactly, because total zeros = sum (q_i * X + r_i) = X * sum q_i + sum r_i. So sum r_i ≡ total zeros (mod X). Since total zeros is invariant, sum r_i mod X is invariant. Similarly, sum of remainders of one runs mod Y is invariant. So that's just a consequence of count invariance, not a new invariant.

But the multiset of run lengths might have a more subtle invariant. Consider the operation as a "swap" of two blocks. This is similar to a sorting network. There is a known result: the reachable strings are exactly those that have the same "multiset of characters" and the same "multiset of run lengths modulo something"? Let's think about the "token" idea again. Suppose we group the string into blocks of size X (for 0) and size Y (for 1), but we are allowed to have partial blocks at the ends. But the string might not be uniquely parsed if the counts are not multiples. For example, if X=2, Y=1, N=3, S="001". It can be parsed as one 0-token (len 2) and one 1-token (len 1) exactly. Perhaps we can define a canonical parsing: always take the first X characters that are 0 as a 0-token, and first Y characters that are 1 as a 1-token, greedily? Ones: at 1,4,5 -> runs: 1,2 -> full blocks: 1+1=2? Wait, 2 ones is full block (since Y=1). So full blocks: run 1:1 -> 1 full block; run 2:2 -> 2 full blocks. Total ones full blocks = 3. So still 3. So it seems the total number of full X-blocks of zeros and full Y-blocks of ones is invariant. What about the operation B? B takes Y ones then X zeros, and swaps them. That would also preserve the number of full blocks? Let's test B on a string. S="100", X=2, Y=1. This is B pattern? B requires Y ones then X zeros: "1" then "00". Yes. Apply B: change S_0..S_{0+X-1}=S_0,S_1 to 0, and S_{0+X}..S_{0+X+Y-1}=S_2 to 1. So S becomes "001". Zeros: 2 -> full blocks: 1. Ones: 1 -> full blocks: 1. So it appears that the number of complete X-blocks of zeros and complete Y-blocks of ones is invariant. Is that always true? Consider a run of zeros of length L. Thus, the number of complete X-blocks of zeros (call it Z) and complete Y-blocks of ones (call it O) is invariant. Also, the total number of zeros and ones is invariant. This suggests that the sequence of "full atoms" (each of size X for 0, size Y for 1) can be reordered arbitrarily by adjacent swaps, provided that the atoms are adjacent in the string. So in this case, the partial block moved along with the swaps. It seems that the partial blocks are "carried" by the full blocks as they swap. This suggests that the string can be uniquely represented by a sequence of "runs" of zeros and ones, and the operation only affects the boundaries of runs. Consider the following: map the string to a sequence of numbers that are the lengths of the runs, but normalized by X and Y. If the left part is empty (a=1 and b=0), then the new 1-block is adjacent to the previous 1-run (if any), so it merges. This is messy.

Maybe we can think in terms of the "excess" of zeros and ones. Let's define for each position i, the "balance" of zeros and ones up to that point. Let B(i) = (#0 up to i) - (#1 up to i). The operation A: it takes X zeros and Y ones, and swaps them. How does B(i) change? For positions inside the block, the counts change. This might not be invariant.

Another idea: the problem might be solved by checking a certain condition on the "multiset of 0-blocks of size X" and "1-blocks of size Y" when we consider the string as a sequence of chunks of size X and Y, but with a sliding window. However, we can align them by considering the string modulo (X+Y). Specifically, we can consider the string as a sequence of "cells" of size X+Y? No.

Let's look at the sample 1: N=9, X=2, Y=1. S=000111001, T=011000011. We transformed S to T. Let's see the runs and full blocks. S: runs: 3 zeros, 3 ones, 2 zeros, 1 one. X=2, Y=1. Zeros: 3+2=5. Full 0-blocks: floor(3/2)=1, floor(2/2)=1 -> total 2. Ones: 3+1=4. Full 1-blocks: floor(3/1)=3, floor(1/1)=1 -> total 4. T: 011000011 -> runs: 1 one, 2 zeros? Runs: "0" (partial), "100" (1 one, 2 zeros), "11" (2 ones), "00" (2 zeros), "1" (1 one). Full 0-blocks: "00" (in 100), "00" (the next). Full 1-blocks: "1" (in 100), "11", "1". So full 0: 2, full 1: 4. After B: "0 100 10 01 1"? Actually sample says: "010010011". Let's parse: 0,1,0,0,1,0,0,1,1. Runs: "0", "1", "00", "1", "00", "11". Full 0: "00", "00" -> 2. Full 1: "1", "1", "11" -> 4. After final A: "011000011": runs: "0", "11", "0000", "11". Full 0: "0000" has 2 full blocks? 4/2=2. Full 1: "11" and "11" -> 2 full blocks? Now, can we characterize the sequence of these full blocks? In S, the full blocks in order of appearance: we traverse the string and whenever we see a run, we break it into full blocks and a remainder. For S: run1 zeros: "000" -> full: "00", rem: "0". run2 ones: "111" -> full: "1","1","1", rem: none. run3 zeros: "00" -> full: "00", rem: none. run4 ones: "1" -> full: "1", rem: none. Maybe another sequence: from 0,1,1,1,0,1, we can swap the last 0 and 1: 0,1,1,1,1,0. Then swap the first 0 and 1: 1,0,1,1,1,0. Then swap 0 and 1: 1,1,0,1,1,0. Then swap 0 and 1: 1,1,1,0,1,0. Then swap 0 and 1: 1,1,1,1,0,0. Still not target. Actually, the target is 1,1,0,0,1,1. Let's see if it's reachable: the sequence has three 1s and two 0s? Wait, target: 1,1,0,0,1,1 has four 1s and two 0s. Source: 0,1,1,1,0,1 has two 0s and four 1s. So same counts. Can we reorder? We can bubble the 0s to the left or right. For example, move the first 0 to the right: 0,1,1,1,0,1 -> swap with next 1: 1,0,1,1,0,1 -> swap with next: 1,1,0,1,0,1 -> swap: 1,1,1,0,0,1. Now we have 1,1,1,0,0,1. This is why the problem is nontrivial. There must be a clever invariant. Let's think about the "weight" of the string defined as the sum of some function over all characters. The net change is: sum_{p=i}^{i+X-1} (f(p,1) - f(p,0)) + sum_{q=i+X}^{i+X+Y-1} (f(q,0) - f(q,1)). We want this to be 0 for all i where the operation is applicable. From 2F(i+1) = 2F(i) - g(i) + g(i+M). (2)
Substitute (1) into (2): 2(F(i) - g(i) + g(i+X)) = 2F(i) - g(i) + g(i+M)
=> 2F(i) - 2g(i) + 2g(i+X) = 2F(i) - g(i) + g(i+M)

So for X=Y, the shift is by X, so parity is preserved if X is even. If X is odd, the shift is by odd, so parity changes. So for X=Y, the invariant might be more complex. But anyway.

Back to general X, Y. We found that the condition for an invariant of the form sum c_p g(p) is g(i+X) = g(i+Y) for all i, and the recurrence. This implies g is periodic with period D = |X-Y|. Also, from the recurrence, g(i+M) = 2g(i+X) - g(i). Since M = X+Y, and g(i+X) = g(i+Y), we can write g(i+X+Y) = 2g(i+X) - g(i). This is a linear recurrence of order 2. For a periodic sequence with period D, this recurrence will force g to be constant if D does not divide something. Let's analyze. Let D = |X-Y|. Assume X > Y. Then D = X-Y. The condition g(i+X) = g(i+Y) implies g(i+D) = g(i). So g is periodic with period D. Now, the recurrence: g(i+X+Y) = 2g(i+X) - g(i). Let i = 0: g(X+Y) = 2g(X) - g(0). But g(X) = g(Y) by periodicity? Actually, g(X) = g(Y) because g(i+X)=g(i+Y) for i=0 gives g(X)=g(Y). So g(X) = g(Y). Also, g(X+Y) = g(2X - D) = g(2X) because period D. So g(2X) = 2g(X) - g(0). This is a condition on the sequence. For g to be nonzero, we need a sequence with period D satisfying this. For example, take X=2, Y=1, D=1. Period 1 means g is constant. Then the condition g(4) = 2g(2) - g(0) becomes g(0) = 2g(0) - g(0) => g(0) = g(0), ok. So constant works. But constant g gives total count invariant, which we already have. For X=2, Y=4, D=2. g periodic with period 2: g = [a,b,a,b,...]. Condition: g(6) = 2g(2) - g(0). g(6)=g(0)=a. g(2)=a. RHS=2a - a = a. Works. So any period 2 works. So we have a non-trivial invariant: the difference between the sum of something on even and odd positions. But for X=2, Y=4, the operation is: swap 2 zeros and 4 ones. The shift for zeros is +4, for ones is -2. So zeros move by 4 (even), ones move by 2 (even). So parity of positions is preserved! Indeed, if both shifts are even, then parity is preserved. So the invariant is the number of zeros on even positions vs odd positions. For X=2, Y=4, zeros move by 4 (even), ones move by -2 (even). So both stay on the same parity. So the counts of zeros on even and odd positions are invariant. Similarly, for any X, Y, the shift for zeros is Y, for ones is -X. So the parity of the position of a character changes by Y mod 2 for zeros, and by X mod 2 for ones. So if X and Y are both even, then shifts are even, so parity is preserved. If X and Y are both odd, then shifts are odd, so parity flips for both. So the number of zeros on even positions might change, but the difference between the number of zeros on even and odd positions? Let's check: if a zero moves by odd, it changes parity. So the number of zeros on even positions decreases by 1 and on odd increases by 1. So the difference (even - odd) changes by -2. So not invariant. But maybe the sum of something else is invariant.

Let's consider the sum over all zeros of (-1)^{position} or something. For operation A, zeros at i..i+X-1 become ones, and ones at i+X..i+X+Y-1 become zeros. The change in sum_{0} (-1)^p is: - sum_{i}^{i+X-1} (-1)^p + sum_{i+X}^{i+X+Y-1} (-1)^p. We want this to be 0. That is exactly the condition with g(p) = (-1)^p. So we need sum_{i}^{i+X-1} (-1)^p = sum_{i+X}^{i+X+Y-1} (-1)^p. This is true if the sum over any X consecutive is equal to the sum over the next Y consecutive. For g(p)=(-1)^p, the sum over an interval of length L is (-1)^i (1 - (-1)^L) / 2. So the condition becomes: (-1)^i (1 - (-1)^X)/2 = (-1)^{i+X} (1 - (-1)^Y)/2. This holds if either X and Y are both even (then both sides 0), or both odd (then 1-(-1)^X = 2, 1-(-1)^Y = 2, so LHS = (-1)^i, RHS = (-1)^{i+X} = (-1)^i (-1)^X. For them to be equal, we need (-1)^X = 1, so X even. But if both odd, (-1)^X = -1, so RHS = -(-1)^i, not equal. So for both odd, it doesn't hold. So the parity invariant only works if X and Y are even. If one is even and one odd, the sum might not be invariant.

But the problem allows any X, Y. So we need a more general invariant. The recurrence we derived is exact: any solution g to the conditions gives an invariant. And we can construct such g for any X, Y. Let's solve the recurrence generally. We need a sequence g(i) for i=0..N-1 such that for all i, sum_{k=0}^{X-1} g(i+k) = sum_{k=0}^{Y-1} g(i+X+k). This is equivalent to: for all i, the sum over the M-length window [i, i+M-1] is twice the sum over the first X. This is a condition on the sequence. We can define g arbitrarily on the first M positions, and then the condition determines the rest? Actually, the condition must hold for all i. It's a linear constraint. For a given N, we can find g that satisfies it. But we want an invariant that works for all N? The invariant should be computable for any N. We can define g(i) based on i mod something. The condition must hold for all i in the range where operations can be applied. If we can find a g that satisfies the condition for all i (as an infinite sequence), then the sum is invariant. The condition is a linear recurrence with constant coefficients: g(i+M) = 2g(i+X) - g(i). And we also have g(i+X) = g(i+Y) for all i? Wait, that came from requiring both A and B. Do we need both? The problem allows both operations. So the invariant must be preserved by both. So we need g such that both conditions hold. As we derived, that implies g(i+X) = g(i+Y) for all i. So g is periodic with period D = gcd(X,Y)? Actually, g(i+X) = g(i+Y) for all i implies g(i + (X-Y)) = g(i). So period divides X-Y. Let D = |X-Y|. So g is periodic with period D. Now, the recurrence g(i+M) = 2g(i+X) - g(i) must also hold. With period D, we can write M = X+Y. Note that X and Y can be expressed in terms of D. Let X = a D, Y = b D? Not necessarily, D divides X-Y, but not necessarily X and Y. For example, X=3, Y=1, D=2. X and Y are not multiples of D. So g is periodic with period 2, but X=3 is not a multiple of 2. So g(i+3) is not necessarily equal to g(i+1). But the condition g(i+X) = g(i+Y) means g(i+3) = g(i+1). So g(i+2) = g(i). So g is periodic with period 2. Now, the recurrence: g(i+4) = 2g(i+3) - g(i). Since g(i+3) = g(i+1), and g(i+4) = g(i) (period 2), we have g(i) = 2g(i+1) - g(i) => 2g(i) = 2g(i+1) => g(i) = g(i+1). So g is constant. So for X=3, Y=1, the only solution is constant g. So no non-trivial invariant of this form. But wait, we might not need the condition for both A and B if we can choose different f for each? The invariant is a single quantity that is invariant under both operations. So it must satisfy both. So g must satisfy both. So for X=3, Y=1, the only g is constant. So the only linear invariant of this form is the total count. But we know that for X=3, Y=1, not all strings with same count are reachable. So there must be a non-linear invariant, or a different kind of invariant.

Let's test X=3, Y=1 with small N. S="0001" (X=3, Y=1). Can we get T="1000"? S has 3 zeros, 1 one. T has 1 one, 3 zeros. Apply A? Need 3 zeros then 1 one. S: "0001" at i=0: S_0,S_1,S_2=0,0,0; S_3=1. Yes! Operation A: change S_0..S_{0+Y-1}=S_0 to 1, and S_{0+Y}..S_{0+Y+X-1}=S_1,S_2,S_3 to 0. So S_0=1, S_1=0, S_2=0, S_3=0. New S="1000". So S can become T. What about S="0001", T="0100"? T has zeros at 0,2,3; one at 1. Is T reachable? S="0001" -> A at i=0 gives "1000". Can we get "0100" from "0001"? Apply A at i=1? S_1,S_2,S_3=0,0,1 -> valid. Change S_1 to 1, S_2,S_3,S_4? But N=4, i=1, i+X+Y-1 = 1+4-1=4 out of bounds. So only i=0 works. So only "1000" is reachable. So "0100" is not reachable. Counts are same. So there is an invariant. What is it? Let's list all strings with 3 zeros, 1 one: 0001, 0010, 0100, 1000. Which are reachable from 0001? We saw 0001 -> 1000. From 1000, can we apply B? B requires 1 one then 3 zeros: "1000" has "1" then "000". So B at i=0: change S_0..S_{0+X-1}=S_0,S_1,S_2 to 0, and S_{0+X}..S_{0+X+Y-1}=S_3 to 1. So S_0=0, S_1=0, S_2=0, S_3=1 -> "0001". So 0001 and 1000 are connected. What about 0010? It has "00" then "1" then "0". No pattern of 3 zeros then 1 one. So no operation possible. So 0010 is isolated. 0100 has "0" then "1" then "00". No pattern. So isolated. So the graph has two components: {0001, 1000} and {0010}, {0100}. So the invariant is not just counts. What distinguishes 0001 from 0010? In 0001, the three zeros are consecutive and at the beginning. In 0010, the zeros are split. The operation can only be applied if there is a run of at least 3 zeros followed by 1 one. So the number of runs of length >= X might be invariant? In 0001, there is one run of length 3. In 1000, one run of length 3. In 0010, runs: 2 zeros, 1 one, 1 zero. So no run of length 3. So the multiset of run lengths might be invariant? But we saw earlier that runs can be split. In the example X=2, Y=1, S="00011" had a run of 3 zeros. After operation, we got "01001" with runs of 1 and 2 zeros. So a run of length 3 was split into 1 and 2. So the run lengths are not invariant. However, the "total number of zeros in runs of length >= X" might be invariant? In 0001, the run of 3 zeros is >=3, so it contributes 3. In 1000, also 3. In 0010, no run of length >=3, so 0. So that distinguishes them. But is that always invariant? For X=2, Y=1, S="00011": run of 3 zeros >=2, contributes 3. After operation, "01001": runs of 1 and 2 zeros. The run of 2 is >=2, contributes 2; run of 1 contributes 0. Total = 2. So not invariant (3 vs 2). So that's not it.

Let's think about the "weight" idea again, but maybe with a different assignment. For X=3, Y=1, we want an invariant that distinguishes 0001 from 0010. Assign to each position a weight such that the sum over 0s minus 1s is invariant. We need g such that sum over 3 consecutive = sum over 1 next? Actually, for A: sum_{i}^{i+2} g = g(i+3) (since Y=1). So we need g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2) = g(i+3). This is a linear recurrence! Let's solve this. We want a sequence g such that for all i, g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2) = g(i+3). This is the Fibonacci-like recurrence. The general solution is g(i) = A * r1^i + B * r2^i + C * r3^i, where r are roots of r^3 - r^2 - r - 1 = 0? Actually, r^3 = r^2 + r + 1. The roots are real? One real, two complex. This gives an infinite family of invariants. We can choose specific values to make it easy. For example, choose g(0)=1, g(1)=0, g(2)=0. Then g(3)=1, g(4)=1, g(5)=2, etc. This is like the tribonacci numbers. Then the sum over the string of g(p) for 0 minus g(p) for 1 (or something) might be invariant. Let's test: For operation A on i: we have zeros at i,i+1,i+2, one at i+3. The change in sum: - (g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2)) + g(i+3) = 0 by the recurrence. So it's invariant! And for operation B: we need the condition for B as well. B requires 1 one then 3 zeros: at i, one; i+1,i+2,i+3 zeros. The change in sum: - g(i) (for the one) + (g(i+1)+g(i+2)+g(i+3)) for the zeros. We need this to be 0, so g(i) = g(i+1)+g(i+2)+g(i+3). But from the recurrence, g(i+1)+g(i+2)+g(i+3) = g(i+4). So we need g(i) = g(i+4). So g must be periodic with period 4. But the recurrence g(i+3) = g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2) does not imply period 4. So we need to choose g that satisfies both: g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2) = g(i+3) and g(i+4) = g(i). Let's see if such a g exists. From the first, g(4) = g(1)+g(2)+g(3). From the second, g(4) = g(0). So g(0) = g(1)+g(2)+g(3). Also, g(1) = g(2)+g(3)+g(4) = g(2)+g(3)+g(0). So g(1) = g(0) + g(2) + g(3). Similarly, g(2) = g(0)+g(1)+g(3). g(3) = g(0)+g(1)+g(2). This is a system. Let S = g(0)+g(1)+g(2)+g(3). From g(0)=g(1)+g(2)+g(3) = S - g(0) => 2g(0) = S. Similarly, 2g(1)=S, 2g(2)=S, 2g(3)=S. So all g(i) = S/2. So g is constant. Then the invariant is just the total count. So for X=3, Y=1, the only linear invariant of the form sum c_p g(p) is the total count. But we know there is a more restrictive condition. So the invariant must be non-linear, or involve more than just a linear combination of independent g's.

Perhaps the invariant is the "multiset of run lengths modulo something" or the "sequence of characters when read in a certain way". Let's look at the failing example for X=3, Y=1: S=0001, T=0010. Both have 3 zeros, 1 one. The difference is the position of the one. In S, the one is at the end. In T, the one is in the middle. The operation A can only move the one to the right by 1? Actually, A takes 3 zeros and 1 one and swaps them. So the one moves left by 3? Let's see: S=0001. A at i=0: zeros at 0,1,2; one at 3. After: one at 0; zeros at 1,2,3. So the one moved from 3 to 0. So the position of the one modulo 3 is invariant? In T=0010, the one is at 2. 2 mod 3 = 2. So they have different residues mod 3. So the invariant is: for each one, its position modulo X? Let's find the positions of ones in S and T. S=000111001: ones at 3,4,5,8. Mod 2: 3%2=1, 4%2=0, 5%2=1, 8%2=0. Residues: {0,1} with multiplicities. T=011000011: ones at 1,2,7,8. Mod 2: 1%2=1, 2%2=0, 7%2=1, 8%2=0. Same residues. So that matches. What about the failing example X=2, Y=2, S=00011, T=00110. S ones at 3,4. Mod 2: 1,0. T ones at 2,3. Mod 2: 0,1. Same. So modulo X doesn't distinguish. What about modulo X+Y? M=4. S ones at 3,4: 3%4=3, 4%4=0. T ones at 2,3: 2%4=2, 3%4=3. Different! So maybe the positions modulo M are invariant? For X=2, Y=2, M=4. S ones mod 4: 3,0. T ones mod 4: 2,3. They are different. So the multiset of positions modulo M might be invariant. Let's test with X=3, Y=1, M=4. S=0001: one at 3. Mod 4: 3. T=0010: one at 2. Mod 4: 2. Different. So the position of the one modulo 4 is invariant? But wait, in S, the one moved from 3 to 0 after operation. 0 mod 4 = 0, not 3. So the position modulo M is not invariant! Because 3 and 0 are different mod 4. So that's not it.

Maybe the invariant is the "relative order" of the characters when considered in blocks of size X and Y. Let's think about the "token" approach again, but with a sliding window. Since the operation swaps a block of X zeros and Y ones, we can think of the string as a sequence of "particles" where each particle is a block of size X (if zero) or Y (if one). But the string can be partitioned into these particles in many ways. However, if we consider the string as a sequence of "supercharacters" of size X and Y, we can align the partition by choosing a starting point. For example, we can partition the string into segments of length X and Y, but they must alternate? Not necessarily.

I recall a known solution for this problem: It uses the concept of "potential" or "energy". Assign to each position a value based on its index modulo (X+Y). For a 0, add X; for a 1, subtract Y. Then the total sum is invariant. Let's test this! For operation A: we take X zeros and Y ones. The zeros have value X each, ones have value -Y each. The sum of the block before: X*X + Y*(-Y) = X^2 - Y^2. After operation: the zeros become ones, so they contribute -Y each; the ones become zeros, so they contribute X each. So the new sum: Y*(-Y) + Y*X? Wait, careful: after operation, there are Y ones and X zeros. The Y ones (originally zeros) now have value -Y each, so total -Y^2. The X zeros (originally ones) now have value X each, so total X^2. So the sum of the block is unchanged! So if we assign value X to each 0 and -Y to each 1, then the total sum over the string is invariant! Let's verify: before block: X zeros (value X each) and Y ones (value -Y each). Sum = X^2 - Y^2. After block: Y ones (value -Y each) and X zeros (value X each). Sum = -Y^2 + X^2. Same! So the total sum S = (number of 0s)*X - (number of 1s)*Y is invariant! But wait, that's just a linear combination of the counts. Since total zeros and total ones are invariant, any linear combination is invariant. So that doesn't give a new invariant.

What if we assign value based on position? For a 0 at position p, assign value a_p; for a 1, assign value b_p. We want the sum over the block to be invariant. That is: sum_{p in first X} a_p + sum_{q in next Y} b_q = sum_{p in first X} b_p + sum_{q in next Y} a_q. So sum_{p} (a_p - b_p) = sum_{q} (a_q - b_q). So the sum of (a-b) over the X zeros equals the sum of (a-b) over the Y ones? No, the equation is: sum_{first X} a_p - sum_{first X} b_p = sum_{next Y} a_q - sum_{next Y} b_q. This must hold for all i. This is exactly the condition we had with g = a-b. So we need g such that sum_{i}^{i+X-1} g = sum_{i+X}^{i+X+Y-1} g. So we are back to finding g.

But maybe we can choose g not on the positions, but on the "blocks". Since the operation swaps a block of X zeros and Y ones, we can think of the string as a sequence of "0-tokens" of size X and "1-tokens" of size Y. If we can partition the string into such tokens, then the operation just swaps adjacent tokens. The invariant would be the sequence of token types. But as we saw, the partition is not unique. But they might not be all 0. So that doesn't work.

Wait, maybe the problem can be reduced to a graph reachability where the state is the string, and we only need to check if the "multiset of run lengths" matches. But we saw a counterexample. Let's re-examine the counterexample X=2, Y=2, S=00011, T=00110. Are they really unreachable? Let's try to find a sequence of operations. S=00011. Operations possible: A at i=1 (0-indexed): S_1,S_2=0,0; S_3,S_4=1,1. A: S_1=1, S_2=0? Wait, X=2, Y=2. Operation A: change S_i..S_{i+Y-1} to 1, and S_{i+Y}..S_{i+Y+X-1} to 0. For i=1, Y=2: change S_1,S_2 to 1. X=2: change S_3,S_4 to 0. So S_1=1, S_2=1, S_3=0, S_4=0. Original S_0=0. So new S = "0 1 1 0 0" = "01100". That's not T. Can we apply another operation? From "01100", we have "0 11 00". B requires 2 ones then 2 zeros. At i=1: S_1,S_2=1,1; S_3,S_4=0,0. B: change S_1,S_2 to 0, and S_3,S_4 to 1. So S_1=0, S_2=0, S_3=1, S_4=1. New: 0,0,0,1,1 = "00011". Back. So no other operations. So indeed unreachable. What about S=00110? Can it reach anything? S=00110. Runs: "00", "11", "0". Check A: need 2 zeros then 2 ones. At i=0: "00 11" -> valid. A at i=0: change S_0,S_1 to 1, S_2,S_3 to 0. So S_0=1, S_1=1, S_2=0, S_3=0. New: 1,1,0,0,0 = "11000". B: at i=2: S_2,S_3=0,0; S_4=1? Need 2 ones then 2 zeros. At i=2: S_2=0 not 1. So only A at i=0. So 00110 connects to 11000. So the component is {00110, 11000}. So the invariant is: the position of the "odd" run. In S=00011, the zeros are together at the left. In T=00110, the zeros are split. So the number of zero runs is different. In S, zero runs: one run of length 3. In T, zero runs: two runs (length 2 and 1). So the number of zero runs changed? In 00011, zero runs: 1. In 01100, zero runs: two runs (length 1 and 2). So number of zero runs increased. In 00110, zero runs: 2. So the number of zero runs is not invariant. But maybe the "total number of zeros in the leftmost run" or something.

Let's think about the "leftmost" character. In S=00011, the leftmost is 0. In T=00110, leftmost is 0. Both start with 0. But the internal structure is different.

Another idea: The operation is equivalent to a rotation of the string by Y positions for zeros and X for ones, but only locally. Actually, there is a known interpretation: the string can be thought of as a sequence of "0"s and "1"s, and the operation is a commutation relation. This is similar to the "braid group" or "sorting network". The reachable strings are those that have the same "multiset of characters" and the same "multiset of block lengths when parsed in a specific way". I remember a problem: "ABC 237 F" is not it. Let's search my memory for "X Y S T" "0 1" "Operation A" "Operation B". I think it's from AtCoder ARC 110 or something. Actually, I recall a problem: "AtCoder Grand Contest 044 B" is about a grid. Not that.

Maybe it's from "AtCoder Regular Contest 105 D" which is about a sequence. No.

Let's try to think algorithmically. We need to determine if S can reach T. Since N is 5e5, we need an O(N) or O(N log N) solution. The operation is local and reversible. We can think of the string as a set of "particles" that move. For operation A, X zeros move to the right by Y, and Y ones move to the left by X. This is a deterministic movement when the pattern is present. If we consider the "trajectories" of each character, maybe they are independent? No, they interact.

Consider labeling each character with its "rank" among identical characters. For example, number the zeros from left to right: 0_1, 0_2, 0_3,... and the ones similarly. What does an operation do to the order? Operation A: the X zeros involved are the last X zeros of a zero run? Not necessarily the last X zeros of the entire string, but of that particular run. However, since zeros are indistinguishable, we can think of the operation as taking X consecutive zeros and Y consecutive ones and swapping them. The relative order of the zeros among themselves is preserved (they just move together). The relative order of the ones is preserved. The only change is the interleaving of zeros and ones. So if we list the sequence of characters (ignoring identicality), the operation just swaps a block of X zeros and Y ones. This is exactly a block swap of a contiguous segment of the sequence. The sequence of types is changed. But the relative order of zeros and ones is not just their positions; it's the order in which they appear. If we write the string as a sequence of types, the operation takes a substring of length X+Y that is all 0s then all 1s, and replaces it with all 1s then all 0s. This is a very specific operation. It does not change the count of 0s and 1s, nor does it change the number of runs? Actually, as we saw, it can change the number of runs.

But note that the operation is exactly: replace "0^X 1^Y" with "1^Y 0^X". This is a string rewriting rule. The set of strings reachable from S is the set of strings reachable by applying this rule and its reverse any number of times. This is a confluent rewriting system? If it is confluent, then the reachable strings are exactly those that reduce to the same normal form. So we can define a normal form and check if S and T have the same normal form. What is the normal form? For a given S, we can apply operations to "simplify" it. How to simplify? The operation moves a block of ones to the left and zeros to the right. So if we always apply the operation when possible, we might push all ones to the left? But we can also do the reverse. So there is no unique normal form unless we choose a strategy.

However, note that the operation is reversible, so the set of reachable strings is a connected component in the graph. We can try to find an invariant that partitions the set of strings into components. From the linear algebra approach, we found that the condition for a linear invariant is a recurrence. The recurrence g(i+M) = 2g(i+X) - g(i) with g(i+X)=g(i+Y) has only the constant solution unless X and Y have a common factor? Actually, we saw that for X=2, Y=2, there is a 2-dimensional space. For X=3, Y=1, the recurrence from A is g(i+3) = g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2). From B, we need g(i) = g(i+1)+g(i+2)+g(i+3) => g(i+4)=g(i). So we need g(i+4)=g(i) and g(i+3)=g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2). This system forces g to be constant? Let's solve: from g(i+4)=g(i), the sequence is periodic with period 4. From g(i+3)=g(i)+g(i+1)+g(i+2). Let the period-4 values be a,b,c,d. Then d = a+b+c. Also, from period 4, g(0)=a, g(1)=b, g(2)=c, g(3)=d. Then g(4)=a = g(1)+g(2)+g(3) = b+c+d. So a = b+c+d. But d = a+b+c, so a = b+c+a+b+c = a+2b+2c => 2b+2c=0 => b+c=0. Also, from g(1)=b = g(2)+g(3)+g(4) = c+d+a. So b = c+d+a. Substitute d=a+b+c: b = c+(a+b+c)+a = 2a+b+2c => 2a+2c=0 => a+c=0. Similarly, g(2)=c = d+a+b = (a+b+c)+a+b = 2a+2b+c => 2a+2b=0 => a+b=0. So a+b=0, a+c=0, b+c=0. This implies a=b=c=0, and d=0. So only trivial. So no non-trivial linear invariant of that form.

But maybe we can use a different kind of invariant. For X=3, Y=1, the reachable strings from 0001 are only 0001 and 1000. What is the common property? In 0001, the zeros are at positions 0,1,2. In 1000, zeros at 1,2,3. The one is at 3 in S, at 0 in T. Notice that the one is always at a position that is a multiple of something? In S, one at 3; in T, one at 0. The difference is 3. The zeros are always consecutive? In both, the three zeros are consecutive. In 0010, the zeros are not all consecutive (they are 00 and 0, separated by a one). So the invariant is: the zeros must be consecutive? But wait, from 0001 we can only get 1000. In both, the zeros are consecutive. What about S=0001, can we get 0100? No, because in 0100, the zeros are not consecutive. So the invariant is that the zeros form a contiguous block. Is that always true for X=3, Y=1? If we start with S=0001, the only operation is A, which swaps the three zeros and one one. The result is 1000, which also has the three zeros consecutive. So the zeros remain consecutive. In general, operation A takes X zeros and Y ones and swaps them. The X zeros are consecutive, the Y ones are consecutive. After swap, the Y ones are consecutive, the X zeros are consecutive. So the operation preserves the property that the X zeros are consecutive and the Y ones are consecutive. But it doesn't preserve that all zeros are consecutive. In S=0001, the zeros are consecutive because there are only 3 zeros. In T=1000, also consecutive. In 0010, the zeros are not consecutive because the one is in between. So the invariant is: the zeros can be partitioned into blocks, but the operation only swaps a block of X zeros with a block of Y ones. If the total number of zeros is not a multiple of X, the leftover zeros form a partial block. In 0001, total zeros=3, X=3, so no leftover. The three zeros are exactly one full block. In 1000, also one full block. In 0010, the zeros are two zeros and one zero, separated by a one. So they are not in a single block. So the invariant is that the zeros can be grouped into full X-blocks, but the partial blocks are at the ends? Actually, in 0010, the zeros are not in a single block, but they could be considered as a block of 2 and a block of 1. The block of 2 is less than X, so it's a partial block. The block of 1 is also partial. So there are two partial blocks separated by a one. In 0001, there is one full block (3) and no partials. In 1000, one full block. So the number of partial zero blocks might be invariant? In 0001, 0 partial zero blocks. In 1000, 0. In 0010, 2 partial zero blocks. So that's an invariant.

For X=2, Y=1, let's check. S=00011: zeros: 3. Full X-blocks: one (2), partial: 1. So one full, one partial. The partial is at the end. T=00110: zeros: 3. Full blocks: one (2), partial: 1. But in T, the partial is at the end as well? T=00110: zeros at 0,1,4. The first two are a full block, the last is a partial. So the partial is at the end. In S=00011, the partial is also at the end (the third zero). So both have one full block and one partial at the end. Yet they are not connected! So the number of partial blocks is not the full story. The difference is that in S, the full block and the partial block are in the same run (consecutive). In T, they are in different runs (separated by ones). So the relative order of the full block and the partial block within the zero sequence is different. In S, the zeros are all together: full, partial (since the run is 3). In T, the zeros are: full, ones, partial. So the partial is to the right of the full block, but separated by ones. In S, the partial is to the right of the full block, but not separated. So the operation can separate them by moving the full block to the right past the ones. In S, if we move the full block to the right past the ones, we get "0 1 1 0 0" = 01100. In 01100, the zeros are: partial (0), then full (00). So the partial is to the left of the full block. In T, the partial is to the right of the full block. So the relative order of the full block and the partial block changed! In S, the order is full, partial (with no ones in between). In 01100, the order is partial, full (with ones in between). In T, the order is full, ones, partial. So the partial moved from being adjacent to the full block on the right, to being on the left, to being on the right but separated. So the partial can move around. So the invariant is not simply the order.

Let's think about the "center of mass" or something. For X=2, Y=1, consider the sum of positions of zeros. S=00011: zeros at 0,1,2. Sum=3. T=00110: zeros at 0,1,4. Sum=5. So the sum changed. Operation A: zeros at 1,2 become 0,1? Wait, S=00011, A at i=1: zeros at 1,2 become ones? Actually, S_1,S_2 are zeros. After A, S_1=1, S_2=0, S_3=0. So zeros after: S_2=0, S_3=0. So zeros moved from 1,2 to 2,3. Sum of positions: before: 0+1+2=3. After: 0+2+3=5. So sum increased by 2. So sum of positions is not invariant. But maybe the sum modulo something is invariant? Mod 3? 3 mod 3 = 0, 5 mod 3 = 2. So not invariant mod 3. Mod 2? 3 mod 2 = 1, 5 mod 2 = 1. So mod 2 it's invariant. For X=2, Y=1, M=3. 3 mod 3 = 0, 5 mod 3 = 2. Not invariant mod 3. But mod 2? 1 and 1. So sum of positions mod 2 is invariant? Let's check: sum of positions of zeros mod 2. In S=00011, sum=3 (odd). In T=00110, sum=5 (odd). In 01100, zeros at 0,2,3: sum=5 (odd). So sum mod 2 is invariant? What about ones? S ones at 3,4: sum=7 (odd). T ones at 2,3: sum=5 (odd). 01100 ones at 1,4: sum=5 (odd). So sum of positions of ones mod 2 is also invariant. So the parity of the sum of positions of zeros (or ones) is invariant for X=2, Y=1. For X=3, Y=1, S=0001: zeros at 0,1,2 sum=3 (odd). T=0010: zeros at 0,1,3 sum=4 (even). So sum mod 2 is not invariant (3 vs 4). But mod 3? 3 mod 3 = 0, 4 mod 3 = 1. So not mod 3. So parity of sum of positions is not invariant for X=3, Y=1.

Maybe the invariant is the sum of positions of zeros modulo (X+Y)? For X=3, Y=1, M=4. S sum=3, mod 4 = 3. T sum=4, mod 4 = 0. So not invariant.

Let's try to find a pattern for the counterexample X=3, Y=1. S=0001, T=0010. Both have 3 zeros, 1 one. The difference is the position of the one. In S, the one is at 3. In T, the one is at 2. In S=00011, the one is at 3 and 4. In T=00110, ones at 2 and 3. The sum of positions of ones: 7 vs 5. Mod 4? 7 mod 4 = 3, 5 mod 4 = 1. Different. So sum of positions of ones modulo 4 is invariant? Let's check: S=00011 ones sum=7. T=00110 ones sum=5. 01100 ones sum=5. So sum of ones positions mod 4? 7 mod 4 = 3, 5 mod 4 = 1. So different. But wait, are 00011 and 00110 in the same component? We said no. So the invariant is the sum of positions of ones modulo 4? For X=2, Y=2, M=4. The sum of positions of ones mod 4: S=7≡3, T=5≡1. So they differ. What about zeros? S zeros sum=3, T zeros sum=5. 3 mod 4 = 3, 5 mod 4 = 1. Different. So the sum of positions of zeros mod 4 is also an invariant. For X=3, Y=1, M=4. S=0001 ones sum=3, T=0010 ones sum=2. 3 mod 4 = 3, 2 mod 4 = 2. Different. So the sum of positions of ones mod M is invariant? For X=2, Y=1, M=3. S=00011 ones sum=7, 7 mod 3 = 1. T=00110 ones sum=5, 5 mod 3 = 2. Different! So for the failing example X=2, Y=1, S=00011 and T=00110, the sum of positions of ones mod 3 is different. But wait, are S and T in the same component? We earlier thought S=00011 could reach T=00110? Let's re-examine X=2, Y=1. S=00011. We applied A at i=1 and got 01001. That has ones at 1,4,5? Wait, "01001": 0,1,0,0,1. Ones at 1,4. Sum=5. 5 mod 3 = 2. Original S ones at 3,4 sum=7≡1. So the sum mod 3 changed! So the sum of positions of ones mod 3 is NOT invariant for X=2, Y=1. Because 7 mod 3 = 1, 5 mod 3 = 2. So that's not it.

But wait, in the operation A for X=2, Y=1, the ones moved from 3,4 to 4,5? Actually, S=00011: ones at 3,4. After A at i=1: S_1=1, S_2=0, S_3=0. So new string: S_0=0, S_1=1, S_2=0, S_3=0, S_4=1. So the pattern is exactly a run of X zeros immediately followed by a run of Y ones. So the operation requires that the distance between a zero-run and a one-run is 0 (they are adjacent). So the operation merges or splits runs. Specifically, it takes a run of zeros of length L and a run of ones of length R, with L >= X, R >= Y. It replaces the last X zeros and first Y ones with Y ones and X zeros. This effectively moves X zeros from the end of the zero-run to the right, and Y ones from the beginning of the one-run to the left. The zero-run becomes length L-X (if L>X) or disappears, and the one-run becomes length R-Y. Then a new run of Y ones is inserted to the left of the new X zeros. So the runs are: (L-X) zeros, Y ones, X zeros, (R-Y) ones. This can merge with adjacent runs. The key is that the operation only affects the boundary between a zero-run and a one-run. It moves X zeros to the right past Y ones, or Y ones to the left past X zeros. So the relative order of the "chunks" of size X and Y can be changed. This is exactly a sorting problem on a sequence of items of two types, where each item has a size. The items are the runs, but they can be split. However, if we consider the string as a sequence of "0-chunks" of size X and "1-chunks" of size Y, and we allow partial chunks, the operation swaps an adjacent 0-chunk and 1-chunk, but if a chunk is partial, it might not be swappable. Actually, the operation always uses a full X-chunk and a full Y-chunk. So partial chunks are "locked" in the sense that they cannot be the X or Y in the operation. But they can be adjacent to full chunks. When a full chunk swaps, it can move past a partial chunk? No, the pattern requires exactly X zeros. If a partial chunk is at the end of a run, the run has length > X. The full X-chunk is taken from the end. The partial chunk is the remainder. So the partial chunk is always at the end of the run (on the side away from the swap). So the partial chunk is like a "tail" that doesn't participate in the swap. As swaps happen, the tail can be left behind or merged. For example, in S=00011, the zero run has a tail of 1 (since 3 = 2+1). The full 0-chunk is at the end (positions 1,2). The tail is at the beginning (position 0). When we swap, the full 0-chunk moves to the right, and the tail stays at the left. So the tail becomes a separate run. In the new string, the tail is a run of 1 zero. The new full 0-chunk is at the right. So the tail and the full chunk are now in different runs. So the tail can be separated from its original full chunk. So the partial chunks are not permanently attached to specific full chunks. This means that the sequence of full chunks and partial chunks can be rearranged in a complex way.

However, note that the total number of full X-chunks and full Y-chunks is invariant. Let Z be the number of full X-chunks of zeros, and O be the number of full Y-chunks of ones. The total zeros is Z*X + r0, where 0 <= r0 < X. The total ones is O*Y + r1, 0 <= r1 < Y. So r0 and r1 are determined by the total counts and Z, O. But Z and O are invariant. So the number of full chunks of each type is invariant. Moreover, the full chunks can be thought of as "items" that can be permuted. The partial chunks are "leftover" characters that are not full. The operation allows swapping an adjacent full 0-chunk and full 1-chunk. This is exactly a bubble sort on the sequence of full chunks. The partial chunks are attached to the ends of the sequence of full chunks? Actually, the partial chunks are always at the very left or very right of the entire string? Not necessarily. In S=00011, the partial 0 is at the left of the full 0-chunk. In 01001, the partial 0 is at the left of the full 1-chunk? Actually, 01001: zeros at 0,2,3. The partial 0 is at 0. The full 0-chunk is at 2,3. So the partial 0 is separated from the full 0-chunk by a 1. So the partial 0 is not adjacent to the full 0-chunk. So the partial chunks can be moved around. But note that the partial chunks are always of size less than X or Y. They can be moved by being part of a run that gets split or merged. In fact, the partial chunks can be thought of as "markers" that are carried along.

Let's think about the "center of mass" again, but with a twist. For each position i, assign a value a_i for 0 and b_i for 1. We want the sum to be invariant. So g is 0 at 0, and c elsewhere. This is a valid g! Let's test: X=2, Y=1, M=3. g = [0, c, c, c, ...]. Then sum over 2 consecutive: i=0: g(0)+g(1)=c. i=1: g(1)+g(2)=2c. i=2: g(2)+g(3)=2c. Not equal. So this g does not satisfy the sum condition! I must have made a mistake. The condition is sum_{i}^{i+X-1} g = sum_{i+X}^{i+X+Y-1} g. For X=2, Y=1: sum_{i}^{i+1} g = g(i+2). So we need g(i)+g(i+1) = g(i+2) for all i. This is the Fibonacci recurrence! The general solution is g(i) = A * r1^i + B * r2^i, where r are roots of r^2 = r + 1? Wait, g(i+2) = g(i) + g(i+1). Actually, for B, we need sum_{i}^{i+Y-1} g = sum_{i+Y}^{i+Y+X-1} g. For Y=1, X=2: g(i) = g(i+1)+g(i+2). So g(i+2) = g(i) - g(i+1). Combined with g(i+2) = g(i)+g(i+1), we get g(i)+g(i+1) = g(i) - g(i+1) => 2g(i+1)=0 => g(i+1)=0 for all i. So g is zero. So indeed, for X=2, Y=1, there is no non-trivial periodic g that satisfies both. But wait, we don't need the condition for B if we can find a different invariant for A? The problem has both operations, so the invariant must be preserved by both. So g must satisfy both. So the only solution is g=0. So no non-trivial linear invariant of the form sum c_p g(p) exists for X=2, Y=1. But we know there is an invariant! So the invariant is not of that form.

What if we assign different weights to 0 and 1, not just g(p) = w(0) - w(1)? We can assign w(0,p) and w(1,p) independently. The change in sum is: for zeros becoming ones: lose w(0,p), gain w(1,p). For ones becoming zeros: lose w(1,q), gain w(0,q). So net change: sum_{p in first} (w(1,p) - w(0,p)) + sum_{q in second} (w(0,q) - w(1,q)). Let f(p) = w(0,p) - w(1,p). Then change = -sum_{first} f(p) + sum_{second} f(q). This is exactly the same as before with g = -f. So it's the same condition.

This is not productive. Let's think differently. The operation is exactly a "rotation" of a block of length X+Y. If we consider the string as a sequence of characters, and we assign each character a "type" and a "value", maybe we can use a potential function. Consider the following: define a sequence of "particles" where each 0 is a particle with mass X, and each 1 is a particle with mass Y. The operation swaps a particle of mass X and a particle of mass Y. This is exactly the "sorting by adjacent transpositions" on particles with masses. The center of mass of the system is invariant? The center of mass is (sum of positions * mass) / total mass. But positions change. The operation A: X zeros at i..i+X-1 move to i+Y..i+X+Y-1. So their center of mass moves from i + (X-1)/2 to i+Y + (X-1)/2. So the shift is Y. For the ones: they move from i+X..i+X+Y-1 to i..i+Y-1. Their center of mass moves from i+X + (Y-1)/2 to i + (Y-1)/2. The shift is -X. So the total center of mass changes by: X*Y (for zeros) + Y*(-X) (for ones) = 0. So the center of mass is invariant! But that's just the sum of positions weighted by mass. Since mass is constant for each character type, this is the same as sum of positions of zeros * X + sum of positions of ones * Y. We already saw that this is not invariant because the change is not zero? Wait, let's compute: change in sum of positions of zeros: the zeros move by Y, so their sum changes by X*Y. The ones move by -X, so their sum changes by Y*(-X) = -X*Y. So the total change in (sum_zero * X + sum_one * Y) is X * (X*Y) + Y * (-X*Y) = X^2 Y - X Y^2 = XY(X-Y). Not zero unless X=Y. So the center of mass is not invariant unless X=Y. So that doesn't work.

Let's think about the "leftmost one" more carefully. Why is its parity invariant? Operation A: the leftmost one can only be affected if it is preceded by X zeros. If it is preceded by X zeros, then it is at position >= X. The operation A takes those X zeros and Y ones starting at that one. But for X=2, Y=2, S=00011 leftmost one at 3, mod 2 = 1. T=00110 leftmost at 2, mod 2 = 0. So that works too. So for these examples, the invariant is the position of the leftmost one modulo X. But is that always the invariant? Let's test with a case where there are more ones. For N=6, X=2, Y=1. Consider a string with two ones: S=000011 (ones at 4,5). Leftmost one at 4, mod 2 = 0. New: 001001? Let's do: N=6. S=000011. i=2 (0-indexed): S_2,S_3=0,0; S_4=1. A: S_2=1, S_3=0, S_4=0. New: 0,0,1,0,0,1 = 001001. Ones at 2,5. Leftmost at 2, mod 2 = 0. So still 0. Let's design the run-length reduction:

  We need to split node1 into left part of length Y and right part of length R-Y.

  typ = [0] * MAX
  ln = [0] * MAX
  nxt = [0] * MAX
  prv = [0] * MAX
  We'll use index 0 for head, 1 for tail. We'll keep a `node_count` starting at 2.
  To create a new node: typ[node_count] = t; ln[node_count] = l; node_count += 1; return node_count-1.
  To insert after p: we can create a new node, set its nxt = nxt[p], prv = p. Then update nxt[p] and prv[nxt[p]].
- Build the initial linked list for S: parse S into runs. For each run, create a node and link it.
  head = 0, tail = 1. Set typ[head] = -1, typ[tail] = -2. Initially, head.next = tail, tail.prev = head.
  Then for each run, create node and insert after the last node. We can keep a pointer `last` starting at head, and after each insertion, set last = new_node.
- To find the next pattern: we can start from head.next, and traverse until we find a node with typ=0, ln >= X, and nxt[node] has typ=1 and ln >= Y. We can use a while loop.
- When found, we have `cur` (the zero-node) and `nxt` = nxt[cur] (the one-node).
- Apply the operation as described.
- After the operation, we need to set `cur` for the next search. We can set `cur` to the first node of the inserted segment, or to the node before the segment. To be safe, we can set `cur` to the node before the segment, and then in the next search, we will move forward. Actually, the pattern might reappear at the previous position. So we should set `cur` to the node that is now at the position of the old `cur` (which might be a new node or the previous node). We can determine this by looking at the first run in the merged segment. If the segment is not empty, we can set `cur` to the first new node. If the segment is empty, we set `cur` to the node after the segment (which is `aft`).
- After we break out of the loop, we have the normal form. We can traverse from head.next to tail (excluding tail) and collect the runs into a list of tuples. We can compare this list with the one from T.

This should work and be efficient. The number of operations is O(N), each taking O(1) amortized. So total O(N).

We need to be careful with the merging. Let's write a helper function to merge a list of (type, len) and return a new list.

Let's code the run-length reduction in a separate function.

I'll write the code in Python. Since N is up to 5e5, we need to be efficient. Using lists for typ, ln, nxt, prv with integer indices is fast in Python if we avoid too many object creations. We can use the `array` module or just lists. Lists are fine.

Let's write the code step by step.

First, the function to reduce a string to normal form:
```python
def reduce_to_normal(s, X, Y):
    # Parse into runs
    runs = []
    if s:
        cur_char = s[0]
        cur_len = 1
        for c in s[1:]:
            if c == cur_char:
                cur_len += 1
            else:
                runs.append((cur_char, cur_len))
                cur_char = c
                cur_len = 1
        runs.append((cur_char, cur_len))
    
    # Initialize linked list
    # We will use a dictionary-based linked list for simplicity? No, let's use arrays.
    # But Python's list append is fast. We can just use a list of nodes and modify next/prev indices.
    # However, we need to insert and remove. We can use a list of nodes where each node is a dict, but dicts are slow.
    # Let's use arrays: typ, ln, nxt, prv.
    max_nodes = 4 * len(s) + 10
    typ = [0] * max_nodes
    ln = [0] * max_nodes
    nxt = [0] * max_nodes
    prv = [0] * max_nodes
    
    head = 0
    tail = 1
    typ[head] = -1
    typ[tail] = -2
    nxt[head] = tail
    prv[tail] = head
    node_count = 2
    
    # Helper to create a node
    def new_node(t, l):
        nonlocal node_count
        typ[node_count] = t
        ln[node_count] = l
        node_count += 1
        return node_count - 1
    
    # Helper to insert after p
    def insert_after(p, t, l):
        new = new_node(t, l)
        nxt[new] = nxt[p]
        prv[new] = p
        prv[nxt[p]] = new
        nxt[p] = new
        return new
    
    # Helper to remove a node (not including sentinels)
    def remove_node(p):
        nxt[prv[p]] = nxt[p]
        prv[nxt[p]] = prv[p]
    
    # Build initial list
    last = head
    for t, l in runs:
        new = new_node(t, l)
        nxt[last] = new
        prv[new] = last
        last = new
    nxt[last] = tail
    prv[tail] = last
    
    # Now reduce
    cur = nxt[head]  # first real node
    while True:
        # Find pattern: cur is zero-run with len >= X, and nxt[cur] is one-run with len >= Y
        # We need to scan from cur onwards. But we might have moved cur back? We can just scan from cur.
        # Actually, we can keep cur as the start of the search. But after operation, the list changes.
        # We'll set cur to the first node of the affected region after each operation.
        found = False
        while cur != tail:
            if typ[cur] == 0 and ln[cur] >= X:
                nxt_node = nxt[cur]
                if nxt_node != tail and typ[nxt_node] == 1 and ln[nxt_node] >= Y:
                    found = True
                    break
            cur = nxt[cur]
        if not found:
            break
        
        # Apply operation at cur
        L = ln[cur]
        R = ln[nxt_node]
        prev_node = prv[cur]
        aft_node = nxt[nxt_node]
        
        # Build the segment runs
        seg = []
        if prev_node != head:
            seg.append((typ[prev_node], ln[prev_node]))
        if L - X > 0:
            seg.append((0, L - X))
        seg.append((1, Y))
        seg.append((0, X))
        if R - Y > 0:
            seg.append((1, R - Y))
        if aft_node != tail:
            seg.append((typ[aft_node], ln[aft_node]))
        
        # Merge adjacent same types
        merged = []
        for t, l in seg:
            if merged and merged[-1][0] == t:
                merged[-1] = (t, merged[-1][1] + l)
            else:
                merged.append((t, l))
        
        # Remove nodes between prev_node and aft_node (exclusive)
        # Actually, we will remove all nodes from cur to nxt_node inclusive, and also prev_node and aft_node will be kept.
        # We can just remove the nodes that are in the range (cur, nxt_node). But we also need to keep prev_node and aft_node.
        # We can remove cur, nxt_node, and any nodes between them. Since we inserted new ones, there are no between.
        # But we also might have other nodes if we didn't remove? No, the list is exactly prev_node -> cur -> nxt_node -> aft_node.
        # So we can remove cur and nxt_node. However, if L-X==0, we don't need cur. If R-Y==0, we don't need nxt_node.
        # But we can just remove them and then re-insert the merged runs between prev_node and aft_node.
        
        # To simplify, we can remove cur and nxt_node, and then insert the merged runs after prev_node.
        # But we need to be careful with aft_node: if aft_node is tail, we need to link to tail.
        
        # Let's do: remove cur and nxt_node.
        # But wait, if we remove them, the links are updated. We can then insert new nodes after prev_node.
        # However, if prev_node is head, we insert after head.
        
        # Actually, we can just remove all nodes from nxt[prev_node] to prv[aft_node]? That would remove everything between prev_node and aft_node.
        # So we can set nxt[prev_node] = aft_node, and prv[aft_node] = prev_node. That removes cur, nxt_node, and any other nodes between them. Since there are no other nodes, it's fine.
        # Then we insert the merged runs after prev_node.
        
        nxt[prev_node] = aft_node
        prv[aft_node] = prev_node
        
        # Now insert merged runs after prev_node
        last_node = prev_node
        for t, l in merged:
            new = new_node(t, l)
            nxt[last_node] = new
            prv[new] = last_node
            last_node = new
        nxt[last_node] = aft_node
        prv[aft_node] = last_node
        
        # Set cur for next search. We want to re-check the region. We can set cur to the first node of the inserted segment, or to prev_node.
        # The pattern might have been at prev_node? If prev_node is head, we set cur to nxt[head]? Actually, we can set cur to the first new node if merged is not empty, else aft_node.
        if merged:
            # The first new node is the one after prev_node
            cur = nxt[prev_node]
        else:
            cur = aft_node
            # But we need to continue searching from here. If aft_node is tail, the loop will break.
        
    # Collect final runs
    result = []
    cur = nxt[head]
    while cur != tail:
        result.append((typ[cur], ln[cur]))
        cur = nxt[cur]
    return result
```

This seems correct. We need to make sure that when we remove nodes, we don't lose the ability to access them. We are just relinking, so the nodes are "leaked" but that's fine. We can reuse them in the new_node function by maintaining a free list, but it's not necessary since the total nodes is bounded by 4N.

So the algorithm works on the examples.

Now, we need to implement this in Python efficiently. The number of nodes is at most initial_runs + 2*operations. Since each operation adds at most 2 new runs, and we remove 2 runs, the net change in number of nodes is 0. Actually, we remove cur and nxt_node, and add up to 4 nodes. But we also might remove prev_node and aft_node? No, we keep them. So we remove 2 nodes and add up to 4 nodes. So the number of nodes can increase by 2 per operation. Since the number of operations is O(N), the total nodes is O(N). So we can preallocate 4N.

We also need to handle the case where X+Y > N. Then no operation can be applied, so the normal form is the string itself. Our algorithm will just scan and find no pattern, so it will return the original runs. That's fine.

Let's code the full solution.

One optimization: we can use `int` for the arrays. The total time should be O(N) if we are careful. In Python, the loop over the linked list might be slow if we do it in Python. But N=5e5, and the number of operations is O(N). Each operation does a constant amount of work. So it should be within the time limit if we use fast operations. We can use local variables for the lists to avoid global lookups.

Let's write the code.

```python
import sys

def reduce_to_normal(s, X, Y):
    if not s:
        return []
    # Parse into runs
    runs = []
    cur_char = s[0]
    cur_len = 1
    for c in s[1:]:
        if c == cur_char:
            cur_len += 1
        else:
            runs.append((cur_char, cur_len))
            cur_char = c
            cur_len = 1
    runs.append((cur_char, cur_len))
    
    n = len(s)
    max_nodes = 4 * n + 10
    typ = [0] * max_nodes
    ln = [0] * max_nodes
    nxt = [0] * max_nodes
    prv = [0] * max_nodes
    
    head = 0
    tail = 1
    typ[head] = -1
    typ[tail] = -2
    nxt[head] = tail
    prv[tail] = head
    node_count = 2
    
    def new_node(t, l):
        nonlocal node_count
        typ[node_count] = t
        ln[node_count] = l
        node_count += 1
        return node_count - 1
    
    # Build initial list
    last = head
    for t, l in runs:
        new = new_node(t, l)
        nxt[last] = new
        prv[new] = last
        last = new
    nxt[last] = tail
    prv[tail] = last
    
    cur = nxt[head]
    while True:
        # Find pattern
        found = False
        while cur != tail:
            if typ[cur] == 0 and ln[cur] >= X:
                nxt_node = nxt[cur]
                if nxt_node != tail and typ[nxt_node] == 1 and ln[nxt_node] >= Y:
                    found = True
                    break
            cur = nxt[cur]
        if not found:
            break
        
        # Apply operation
        L = ln[cur]
        R = ln[nxt_node]
        prev_node = prv[cur]
        aft_node = nxt[nxt_node]
        
        # Build segment
        seg = []
        if prev_node != head:
            seg.append((typ[prev_node], ln[prev_node]))
        if L - X > 0:
            seg.append((0, L - X))
        seg.append((1, Y))
        seg.append((0, X))
        if R - Y > 0:
            seg.append((1, R - Y))
        if aft_node != tail:
            seg.append((typ[aft_node], ln[aft_node]))
